MC	<p>5c14_5a40</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- GATGATCGT --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>2fe3_fdb8</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TAGGGACGC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>85a8_a3d9</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- AACCAGTGC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>7f46_56bf</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. MC	<p>7c80_4be0</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- CCGATCGCA --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>01b3_bdb5</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TCAATCGAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>cd92_6ebd</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TTGACAAAC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>7cfd_cab6</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- AGTCCAACC --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>4ea0_6d82</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- ACTGTTGGG --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse; MC	<p>ee1e_d084</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. MC	<p>e301_ee1f</p> <p>DNA is organized as a double helix where two strands run in opposite, or anti-parallel, directions. The ends of these strands are referred to as 5&prime; (five prime) and 3&prime; (three prime) ends. The strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary base pairs. Specifically, <span style="color: darkgreen;"><strong>adenine (A)</strong></span> forms a pair with <span style="color: darkred;"><strong>thymine (T)</strong></span>, and <span style="color: dimgray;"><strong>guanine (G)</strong></span> pairs with <span style="color: darkblue;"><strong>cytosine (C)</strong></span>. This base pairing is critical for the stability of the DNA molecule and is central to processes like DNA replication and transcription.</p><br/><!-- TCTGTCAGG --> &nbsp;<table style="border-collapse: collapse;
